On the real exit path the mpv engine only performed a soft stop and left the actual libmpv teardown to a window-destroy event, so if that event was delayed or the frame wasn’t destroyed, audio could outlive the app. The player controller now hard-terminates the engine deterministically on shutdown, and the window-destroy handler is guarded so a child window’s destruction can’t tear it down early; termination is idempotent. (The “keeps running in the tray” facet of #1195 is the deliberate close-to-tray behavior established in #1193 — Ctrl+W and the X minimize by design; use Exit to quit.) (ui/radio/mpv_radio_engine.py, ui/radio/player_controller.py)

  • New Folder (Ctrl+Shift+E) works from the Quill Radio favorites tree (#1211). The Station menu advertised the accelerator, but a focused favorites TreeCtrl could swallow the chord before the menu accelerator fired, so nothing happened. Ctrl+Shift+E is now handled in the app char hook (like the reorder chord) so it opens New Folder app-wide, regardless of focus. (apps/radio.py::_on_radio_char_hook)

  • Delete all radio favorites in one step (#1201). The Favorites manager gains a Remove All… button that clears every favorite (folders are kept) after a confirmation that defaults to No; a backup is snapshotted on save so it can be undone. (ui/radio/favorites_manager_dialog.py, ui/radio/favorite_actions.py::remove_all_favorites, core/radio/favorites.py::clear)

  • Quill Radio favorites move up/down works from the default view without destroying a hand-arranged order (Quill Radio 2.2.0, #1186). Reordering from a sorted (A-Z/Z-A) view is treated as an intent to hand-arrange: the sort flips to manual, the tree reloads to reveal the preserved stored order, and the move happens within that now-visible order (announced “Switched to manual order”). Crucially it no longer bakes the alphabetized view over the stored list — which used to silently overwrite a listener’s hand-arranged order the first time they reordered from A-Z, with no way to recover it. The stored order is left untouched; only the sort setting changes. (apps/radio.py::_force_favorites_manual_order)

  • Quill Radio Exit really exits (Quill Radio 2.2.0, #1193). An explicit menu/tray Exit called the same close path as the titlebar X, so with close-behavior set to “minimize to tray” it bounced right back into the tray and the app could never be closed. A shared _exit_application() now flags the request so the close handler quits for real, bypassing minimize-on-close and the accidental-Alt+F4 confirm. (ui/app_shell.py, apps/radio.py)

  • Quill Radio menu bar is reachable on launch (Quill Radio 2.2.0, #1193). The pre-show SetFocus did not stick, so the window opened with no control focused and the first Alt opened the window system menu instead of the app menu bar; focus now lands on the favorites tree via wx.CallAfter after the frame is shown. (apps/radio.py)

  • Quill Radio NOAA state folders show the stations they promise (Quill Radio 2.2.0). A Weather / NOAA state could show a count (“9 items”) yet expand to nothing, because the count came from the live /v1/states endpoint while the leaves come from the full-directory tier. wxindex.states_with_playable_feeds now derives the folders and counts from that same tier, so a count always equals what expanding shows. (core/radio/wxindex.py)

  • Quill Radio “Install and restart” no longer refuses the update (quill-radio 2.1.2, #1183). One-click self-update extraction shares the generic 512 MiB decompression-bomb guard, but a full portable app bundle (Python + ffmpeg + mpv + libraries) uncompresses well past that – Quill Radio’s own portable zip is ~422 MiB compressed and over 1 GiB expanded – so the extractor refused the legitimate update and Install-and-restart failed with “total uncompressed size exceeds 536870912 bytes (possible decompression bomb)”. The update extractor (which only ever runs on Quill’s own HTTPS + trusted-host-verified release asset, and keeps the per-entry compression-ratio guard) now uses a bundle-sized 4 GiB ceiling. (core/updates.py::UPDATE_MAX_UNCOMPRESSED)

  • Quill Radio favorites keep their hand-arranged order on upgrade (quill-radio 2.1.2, #1168, #1178). The Favorites sort-order setting added in 2.0.2 defaulted an absent key (a store written by an earlier version, favorites in the user’s manual order) to Ascending A-Z – silently re-sorting 30-plus carefully ordered stations on the first launch after updating. A missing key is now treated as “manual” so an existing order is preserved; only an explicit stored value re-sorts. (The display sort is non-mutating, so anyone already re-sorted by the old default can restore their order with Unsorted in Preferences.) (core/radio/history.py)

  • Speech Hub no longer crashes opening Dictation (Offline) (#1135, #1177). SpeechSetupDialog gained required kokoro_ok / kokoro_can_install arguments on 2026-06-26, but the Speech Hub’s offline dictation panel did not pass them until 0.9.0 beta 3, so 0.9.0 beta 2 raised TypeError: SpeechSetupDialog.__init__() missing 2 required keyword-only arguments. Fixed in beta 3 and now guarded by a source-contract test that fails if any required SpeechSetupDialog kwarg is ever dropped again. (ui/speech_hub_dialog.py, ui/main_frame.py)

  • NOAA Weather Radio Browse no longer shows empty states. With a network connection, expanding a state in the Browse Stations “Weather / NOAA” branch showed nothing and moved to the next state – every state, because the live WeatherIndex per-state endpoint returns only a feed count, not the feed URLs (those live in the full directory / bundled snapshot), so every station parsed online had no playable stream and was filtered out. Browse now sources a state’s playable transmitters from the full-directory tier (the refreshed directory cache, else the bundled snapshot – both of which carry feeds), hides states that have no re-streamed transmitter at all, and labels each state with its playable count. Works online and offline, and needs no per-state network call. (core/radio/wxindex.py::playable_stations_for_state)

  • Radio Reading Services shows the full curated list, not a shrunken one. The list could collapse to ~7 services because the resolver mirrored NOAA’s “live replaces snapshot” model, letting a narrow RadioBrowser keyword search stand in for the 20 hand-vetted bundled services (most of which – ACB Media, WRBH, Sun Sounds – are not literally named “radio reading” and so never come back from that search). The bundled curated list is now always shown, with any live-discovered services unioned on top and de-duped by stream URL; the update command reports the total you will actually see. (core/radio/reading_services.py)

  • iHeart Browse ordering and live favorites. In the iHeart genre tree, the “0-9” bucket now sorts before “A” (it was after “Z”) and stations within each letter are alphabetised. And adding a favorite while the Browse Stations window is open now updates its Favorites branch immediately instead of only when the window is reopened. (ui/radio/browse_tree_dialog.py)

  • The Source filter in station search shows a station under every directory it appears in. A SomaFM channel that RadioBrowser also lists was de-duplicated to the RadioBrowser copy in the unified search, so picking SomaFM in the Source dropdown hid it (while TuneIn’s copy, which resolves a different mount URL, survived and showed under TuneIn). The merge now records every directory that carried a de-duplicated station, and the Source filter matches against the full set – so Groove Salad appears under SomaFM, RadioBrowser, and TuneIn alike. The result row still badges the winning source. (core/radio/directory_search.py, transient RadioStation.alt_sources)

  • Radio recordings survive a restart, and ask before resuming (R3). A recording in progress used to be lost if QUILL (or Quill Radio) quit or crashed – the ffmpeg child kept writing to a temp file nobody would ever find. QUILL now persists an active-recording marker at start and clears it on a clean stop, so a crash leaves a breadcrumb. On the next launch, QUILL reconciles the temp folder (moves any finished orphan files to your recordings folder, leaves a file still being written untouched) and, if a recording was in progress and is still within a 10-minute grace window, asks once: “A recording of WQXR was in progress until 9:00 AM. Resume it for the remaining 12 minute(s)?” with Resume (Enter), Skip (Escape), and a Don’t ask me again checkbox that remembers your choice (always resume / never resume, changeable later in Preferences). Choosing Resume restarts the recording for the remaining minutes only. The marker is strict-discard on corrupt data, so a bad marker file can never drive a bogus resume.

  • Scheduled recordings fire reliably throughout their window, not just at the exact minute (R2). The scheduler used to match the exact start minute, so a recording scheduled for 8:00 that the app reached at 8:01 (after a slow launch or a brief stall) silently never fired, and a failed start still burned the occurrence for the day. The scheduler now uses a next-due-timestamp window model: an entry is due from its start through the end of its duration (start + duration), so a late arrival starts with the remaining minutes and launch-time catch-up is free. Last-fired is stamped only on a successful start (a failure retries on the next poll within the window), once-only entries auto-disable after they fire, and a same-minute conflict (two schedules due while the recorder is busy) defers the second instead of burning it. The scheduler thread is now lock-guarded and wrapped in a catch-log-continue so it can never die silently, and embedded QUILL gets the same missed-recording report standalone Quill Radio already had.

Improved

  • Offline dictation is more robust and honest. whisper.cpp now accepts a vocabulary hint (--prompt), so the new dictation profile can actually steer it. A latent voice-activity-detection bug — Conversation Mode built its silence detector with the sample rate multiplied by the channel count, correct only because capture happens to be mono — is fixed, so end-of-turn timing stays right if capture ever becomes multi-channel. And the legacy Windows-dictation note that claimed “QUILL does not yet capture or transcribe audio itself” was long out of date and is corrected: QUILL has a full offline recognizer (Locked Dictation, core/speech/dictation/), and that Win+H shim is only the OS-panel fallback. (core/speech/providers/whispercpp.py, ui/main_frame_speech_voice.py, core/dictation.py; see docs/design/617-audit.md)

  • PDF text is repaired before you read it, not after you give up on it. A PDF stores where each letter was printed, not sentences, so what an extractor hands back is a picture of the page: words split by a hyphen at the end of every line, each printed line its own paragraph, headings spelled out one letter at a time, and ligatures stored as characters that mean nothing outside the document’s own font — which a screen reader reads as silence, so “office” is announced as “o ce”. QUILL now repairs all four as it opens the file. Hyphen-split words are rejoined (“inter-” + “national” becomes “international”) while real hyphens are kept (Anglo-Saxon, 2018-2019); hard-wrapped lines are reflowed into paragraphs, so speech runs to the end of the sentence instead of stopping at the edge of the page, while blank lines, list items, headings, and finished sentences keep their breaks; letter-spaced display titles (“C H A P T E R O N E”) collapse to real words that Find can match, leaving ordinary text such as “A B testing” alone; and private-use ligature codepoints are turned back into their letters, with unrecoverable ones dropped. Every heuristic is conservative — where a case could go either way the text is left exactly as found, already-clean text comes through unchanged, and nothing is ever joined across a page break, so Go To Page stays exact. (io/pdf_repair.py, io/pdf.py)

  • Word documents now read properly out of the box (#1279). A Word file opened in a packaged install used to come through QUILL’s weakest reader — a hand-rolled XML walk that produced one flat line per paragraph, with headings, lists, and tables all flattened away — because the better reader was an optional download most people never found. Word extraction now goes through python-docx, which every install already has: headings arrive as heading levels, bulleted and numbered lists as lists, and tables as tables, in document order. Nothing to download, no size cost. The old XML walk stays as a last resort for a file python-docx cannot open. (On the Mac this needed a packaging fix too — python-docx is imported lazily, so py2app was not bundling it at all and the .app had only the raw-XML floor.)

  • PDF text and Excel sheets work in a fresh install (#1279). The Windows installer and portable build, and the macOS .app, now bundle pdfplumber, pypdf, and openpyxl (about 16 MB) — without them a new install had no PDF text reader at all and could not open an .xlsx, and there was nothing to tell you why. The full MarkItDown pack stays a one-click download in Help > Download Optional Components rather than being bundled: it drags in an ONNX runtime, NumPy, and pandas (roughly 150 MB installed) for a better-but-not-required reader, which is not a fair tax on everyone’s download. If a document does land on a reduced reader, the open announcement and the Document Intake Report now say so in one line and point at that download, instead of leaving you to guess why the text looks thin.

  • Recordings list stops flickering and keeps your place (R1). The Recordings manager used to rebuild its whole list every refresh, snapping a screen reader back to the top and losing your selection. It now updates rows in place keyed by full path: a no-op when nothing changed, selection/focus/scroll preserved when it has, and status flips update the existing row instead of rebuilding it. The active recording is counted from the recorder itself (so a recording writing to a temp folder is no longer invisible), a firing schedule is no longer double-counted, completed one-time entries drop out of the scheduled count, the active row shows a live elapsed time, scheduled entries show their zone-labeled times, and the tray tooltip carries “(recording)”.

  • Radio recordings harden against drops, dead streams, and a crashed host (R4). Four pipeline fixes: (1) a reconnect now records only the remaining time to the original scheduled end, not a fresh full duration – a 60-minute show that drops at minute 50 records a ~10 minute continuation, not another 60. (2) Output filenames are uniquified (“ (2)“, “ (3)” …) instead of silently overwriting – the unconditional -y is gone, so a pattern without a {time} token no longer destroys an earlier recording of the same name, and continuation parts keep the original start timestamp in their name so they group together. (3) A drop is now classified fatal (disk full, HTTP 4xx such as a 404/410 the server took down) vs transient (a network hiccup or 5xx); a fatal drop stops spending reconnect attempts instead of spamming continuation files for a stream that is gone. (4) On Windows, ffmpeg is launched in a job object that dies with QUILL, so a crashed or killed host can no longer strand a bare ffmpeg writing to your temp folder; and the stop-and-wait fallback moved off the UI thread, so closing the window never hangs on a slow ffmpeg shutdown.

  • Quill Radio tells you about recordings it missed while it was closed (quill-radio #4). The scheduler only records while the app is running, so a scheduled recording whose time passed while Quill Radio was closed used to disappear silently. On launch, Quill Radio now checks which enabled schedules (one-time, daily, or weekly, honoring each entry’s time zone) came due during the time it was closed and announces a short summary – “2 scheduled recordings were missed while Quill Radio was closed: WQXR at Tue Jul 14 8:00 AM; …” – naming up to three and collapsing the rest to a count. Nothing is announced when nothing was missed (or on a first run), and a long-dormant install is capped so it can’t recite months of daily occurrences.

Improved

  • Quill Radio now writes a log, and you can choose where (quill-radio #5). The standalone app never configured file logging, so debug mode had nowhere to write; it now writes quill.log from launch (to <data>/logs by default), and Preferences (Ctrl+,) gains a Log folder field to point it wherever you like – changing it moves the live log immediately, and a bad path is ignored rather than breaking logging. This completes the observable-radio work: verbose debug mode, ffmpeg stderr capture, discovery/engine/finalization logging, and settable temp/log locations all now land in one readable log.

  • More radio debug logging: playback-engine selection and recording finalization (quill-radio #5). With radio debug mode on, quill.log now also records which playback backend was chosen for a station and why (preference + whether mpv is present), and how each recording ended and where its finished file landed. Internal cleanup rode along: the pure ffmpeg/ffprobe command and filename builders moved out of recording.py into a new recording_commands module (no behavior change; the recorder module dropped back under its size budget).

  • Radio stream discovery now leaves a debug trail (quill-radio #5). With radio debug mode on, Find Streams from a Website logs each scanned page and the candidate links it kept (with the reason for each, including the iHeart/TuneIn portal-page follow), and the Triton/StreamTheWorld resolver logs which callsign resolved to how many playable streams – so a “why didn’t this station’s stream show up?” question has an answer in quill.log. Every logged URL is redacted first. (More per-module coverage – engine selection, reconnects, recording finalization – follows with the recording-module decomposition.)

  • Radio recordings can use a temporary folder while in progress (quill-radio #5). Recording Settings gains an optional Temporary folder (while recording): when set, a recording is written there and moved to your destination folder the moment it finishes, so a half-written file never appears in your recordings folder and a fast scratch/SSD volume can absorb the write churn. The move is atomic on the same volume and falls back to copy-then-delete across volumes; if the move ever fails the finished file is left safely in the temp folder rather than lost, and an unusable temp folder simply records straight to the destination. Blank keeps today’s behavior (record straight to the destination).

  • Radio recordings now write ffmpeg’s diagnostics to the log (quill-radio #4/#5). A recording’s ffmpeg process used to send its stderr to the void (stderr=DEVNULL), so when a recording failed or produced an odd file there was nothing to look at. Its live stderr is now drained to quill.log on a dedicated reader thread (so it can never fill the pipe buffer and stall ffmpeg): error-shaped lines are logged at WARNING so a failing recording leaves a trail even without debug mode, everything else at DEBUG, and every line is redacted first in case a stream URL carries a token. With radio debug mode on, the recording also runs at ffmpeg’s -loglevel verbose, so the whole connection-and-codec story lands in the log. Groundwork toward the fuller scheduled-recordings and observable-radio work still in progress.

  • Radio recordings: a visible folder by default, and the list stays readable (quill-radio #4). New recordings now go to ~/Music/Quill Radio Recordings (falling back to your home folder) instead of a buried AppData path you had to go hunting for. The Recordings list also stops auto-rebuilding while you’re reading it – the 2-second live refresh pauses whenever the list has keyboard focus and resumes when you move off it, so a screen reader no longer gets yanked back to the top mid-read (the Refresh button still updates on demand).

  • Low-vision: the favorites tree is legible again (quill-radio #3). The saved-stations tree (on the Quill Radio main page and in Manage Favorites) now pins itself to your desktop’s system window and text colours, so it honours your theme and Windows High Contrast mode instead of a bare default that could render near-invisibly. This is a contrast fix, not a dark-mode or theme setting.

  • Browse Stations: Country and Tag/genre are now dropdowns, not free text (quill-radio #2). The Country field is a pickable list (with an “Any country” default) and Tag/genre is an editable combo box — both filled from RadioBrowser’s own most-popular lists (fetched once per session, in the background) so you no longer have to guess the exact spelling of a country or genre. Tag stays editable so a rare custom tag still works, the station name stays free text, and picking a country or tag runs the search right away.

New

  • Unified Find Stations: one search across every source, merged and filterable. Browse Stations now folds all its sources into a single ranked list: results from RadioBrowser, iHeart, TuneIn, and SomaFM are de-duplicated (the same stream on two directories, or the same station name+country twice, collapses to one entry) and exact name matches float to the top. Each result carries a Source column, and a new Source dropdown filters the list to just one source without re-searching. A Refresh Directory button re-fetches iHeart’s station directory (its two-request sitemap index) on demand, for when the catalog has grown since you opened the dialog — TuneIn and RadioBrowser are always live. And you can now type (or paste) a website address right into the search box: Browse Stations scans it for streams and shows what it finds badged as “Website”, so the “Find Streams from a Website” capability lives in the same one search box (the dedicated button stays as a shortcut). Builds on the wx-free directory_search.merge_and_rank blend.

  • Radio verbose logging is now a Preferences checkbox (quill-radio #5). The debug-mode logging control shipped as a foundation with no way to turn it on; standalone Quill Radio’s Preferences (Ctrl+,) now has a Verbose logging (debug mode) checkbox. Turn it on and the radio logger subtrees drop to DEBUG and recording runs ffmpeg at -loglevel verbose, so a hard-to-reproduce playback, recording, or stream-recovery problem leaves a full trail in quill.log; it takes effect immediately (not just next launch) and persists across launches. Off by default (it is chatty).

  • Observable radio: a debug-mode logging control (quill-radio #5, foundation). New quill/core/radio/radio_logging.set_radio_debug() raises just the radio logger subtrees (quill.core.radio/quill.ui.radio) to DEBUG, never the root, so verbose radio diagnostics compose cleanly when Quill Radio is embedded in full QUILL and land in the existing rotating quill.log. The raw-capture codec probe also now records ffprobe’s own (redacted) stderr when it can’t read a codec, instead of silently falling back. (Settable temp/log paths and full per-module logging coverage are follow-on work.)

  • Browse Stations now searches iHeart and TuneIn too. A station search in the Browse Stations dialog fans out to iHeart and TuneIn alongside RadioBrowser and SomaFM and blends the results into one list, each non-RadioBrowser row naming where it came from (“… - via iHeart”/“via TuneIn”). TuneIn results resolve their real stream on the fly; iHeart matches come from its sitemap directory (fetched once per session) with each match’s stream resolved lazily, so a search stays cheap. Both are bounded to a handful of resolved results per search and are failure-tolerant — a down source never blanks the list — and both are off entirely in Safe Mode. Builds on the iHeart/TuneIn core clients above; the wx-free blend helpers live in quill/core/radio/directory_search.py.

  • Set your Read Aloud voice engine straight from Download Optional Components. The Help > Download Optional Components hub gained a Set as Default button for voice engines (Kokoro and the other Read Aloud engines): select a voice-capable component and press it to make that engine — and a voice you pick from it — the Read Aloud default, without opening a separate speech dialog. The button only lights up for components that can actually be a Read Aloud default and once the component is installed. Voice previews also stop cleanly now when you move on to another before the first finishes.

  • iHeart and TuneIn are now station sources for Internet Radio (core clients). Two long-standing gaps in station discovery are filled with open, no-key, no-auth backends that resolve only what the user searched for or what a page already advertises — the same shape as the Triton resolver QUILL already ships, and never a scrape of anyone’s data files. iHeart (quill/core/radio/iheart.py) reads iHeart’s public XML sitemap — two GETs (the index, then the livestations sub-sitemap) yield the whole ~4,790-station list, and each station’s real stream (a revma.ihrhls.com HLS or a StreamTheWorld redirect) is extracted lazily from its page on play, so a refresh is cheap and no bulk crawl ever runs. TuneIn (quill/core/radio/tunein.py) uses RadioTime’s OPML API for live search, browse, and resolving a station’s guide id to its playable stream, and can turn a pasted TuneIn station page into a resolvable station. Both are refused in Safe Mode, funnel through one reviewed egress site each, and stamp results with a source label (a new field on RadioStation) for the upcoming unified Find Stations view. This reverses the earlier “TuneIn/iHeart deliberately left out” non-goal (approved 2026-07-17; PRD §5.84f and the RadioBrowser module docstring updated to match). The station-browser wiring that surfaces these sources in the UI is a separate, following change.

Fixes

  • Report a Bug: paste your whole report, not a 120-character summary (#1102). The short field is now labelled Title (raised to 200 characters) and the long field Description, so it’s clear the short field is just the issue title and the full report goes in Description – rather than users hitting the old “Summary” field’s 120-character cap and having nowhere to put the detail (thank you, Shane). Needs the bundled feedback-hub 1.0.2, which makes the same rename in its own default form and matches the GitHub issue-title length so the full Description always reaches the issue body.

  • Crash-recovery reports are now actionable, and a slow-but-normal shutdown no longer files a false one (#1079, #1085, #1095). Two changes to the “Quill detected an unclean exit” recovery flow. First, when a real crash left a crash-<timestamp>.txt traceback behind, that traceback is now stitched into the auto-submitted recovery report — so a report carries the actual error instead of being log-only. Second, QUILL’s own last-resort hard-exit watchdog logs its force-exit at ERROR level; on a committed shutdown that merely ran slow, that line used to read as a crash and offer recovery next launch with nothing behind it. The clean-exit marker is now written first thing on close (before the slower teardown steps), and the watchdog’s own force-exit line no longer counts as crash evidence, so a slow-but-normal close stops producing empty recovery reports.

  • Check for Updates now downloads the installer for an installed build, not the portable ZIP (#1100). The updater decides between the installer .exe and the portable .zip by detecting how you’re running QUILL, but an installed build that happens to keep its data under {app}\data looked identical to a portable bundle (quill.exe + a data folder), so some installed users were handed the portable ZIP. Detection now also checks for the Inno Setup uninstaller (unins000.exe) that every installed build has and a portable extracted ZIP never does — the same signal the standalone Quill Radio/Cast apps already used — so an installed build reliably gets the installer and a portable build the ZIP.

  • “Go to Entry in Notebook” no longer crashes (#1109). Opening the notebook entry navigator raised TypeError: unhashable type: 'NotebookEntry' — the tree navigator kept an internal lookup keyed by each node’s payload, which fails when the payload is an unhashable object like a notebook entry. It now keys that lookup by the tree item itself (always hashable), so any payload type works. Affects every navigator that carries object payloads, not just notebooks.

  • Find Streams from a Website now fully resolves TuneIn station pages to a playable stream. A scanned (or pasted, or linked-to) tunein.com/radio/... page carries no stream in its HTML – it’s served by RadioTime’s keyless OPML API, keyed by the page’s s-prefixed guide id. Find Streams now recognizes a TuneIn page (by its host), reads the guide id from the URL, and resolves the real stream through that API, the TuneIn counterpart of the iHeart/Triton resolution already in place (#1087). A directory page that merely links to a TuneIn station is followed one level like any portal link, and the resolution happens on the TuneIn page itself; a bare s-id on an unrelated site is never mistaken for a TuneIn station. Refused in Safe Mode; any resolution failure degrades to the normal scan.

  • Find Streams from a Website now resolves iHeart (and TuneIn) station pages to a playable stream instead of returning the page URL (#1087). Scanning a site that links to an iHeart /live/... player page used to hand back the iHeart page URL itself — which does nothing when you press play — because /live is a stream-shaped path hint that short-circuited the scanner before it followed the link. iHeart/TuneIn portal page links are now followed one level deep like any “Listen Live” link, so the real stream embedded in the page’s inline player (an stream.revma.ihrhls.com/.../hls.m3u8 HLS URL, for instance) is extracted and offered instead. A station’s own /live/ path on its own domain is unaffected.

Improved

  • What’s Playing now falls back to the station’s own now-playing endpoint when the stream sends no title (quill-radio #1111). Some stations send no ICY StreamTitle and expose no in-band title, so “What’s Playing” (Ctrl+T) and announce-on-track-change had nothing to report. When both of those come up empty, Quill Radio now reads the current track from the station server’s own Icecast/SHOUTcast status endpoint (/status-json.xsl, /stats, /7.html) on the same host it’s already streaming — no third party, no cost, refused in Safe Mode. This is the free tier of the recognition request; an optional acoustic-fingerprinting tier (a paid/BYO-key service like ACRCloud or AudD) is tracked separately. A literal Shazam integration isn’t possible on Windows (ShazamKit is Apple/Android-only).

  • Scheduled radio recordings: 12-hour times, Edit/Duplicate/Enable, and per-entry time zones (quill-radio #7). The Schedule Recording dialog now accepts a friendly time like 7:30 PM as well as 19:30; you can Edit a schedule in place, Duplicate one as the starting point for a variation (the ACB-convention “same show, several days” case), and Enable/Disable a schedule without deleting it — from buttons or the list’s context menu. Each schedule can also carry its own time zone, so a Pacific listener can pin an Eastern show to Eastern time and it records at the right local moment (DST handled automatically); leaving the zone as “(local time)” keeps the previous behavior. Existing schedules load unchanged (no zone = local). This is radio source shared with the standalone Quill Radio.

  • Internet Radio now identifies itself by name in station logs (quill-radio #6). Playback (mpv) and recording/probing (ffmpeg) used to reach stations as the generic libmpv/Lavf, so a station owner couldn’t tell Quill Radio listeners apart in their logs. Both now send a Quill Radio/<version> User-Agent through one shared helper (quill/core/http_client.py), and the standalone Quill Radio app reports its own release version instead of the embedded QUILL version. The User-Agent carries no user-identifying information, and no new outbound hosts are added.

  • Manage Favorites: Move Up/Down now says where the station landed (quill-radio #1). Reordering a favorite used to announce a bare “Moved down” with no context. It now names the neighbor it now sits next to — “Moved down, now above Delilah Stream” — reusing the same station labels the Mark-and-Move path already speaks. At a folder edge it names the other neighbor instead, so there’s always a reference point.

  • Audio Studio: shared listening modules + workbench player contract. The Chapter Workbench player gained a Mute button (Ctrl+M in the standalone shell) and public transport (play/pause, stop, next/previous chapter) so a host can drive it. open_book_in_workbench now accepts optional on_player_ready / on_finished / on_volume / on_mute / on_closed callbacks threaded through to PlayerPanel — embedded QUILL passes none (unchanged), and the standalone QUILL-AS Audio Studio shell wires them to route media keys, persist per-book volume/mute, stamp Recently Played, and auto-advance a play queue on finish-then-close. The new wx-free stores quill/core/audio_studio/{book_prefs,history,library,play_queue,sleep_timer}.py and the shared library_tree, play_queue_dialog, and sleep_timer_dialog UIs live in quill/ and are vendored into QUILL-AS; each save_* site is classified in the persistence audit and every focusable control carries an accessible name. The two Workbench helper dialogs (silence params, ACX result) moved to chapter_workbench_dialogs.py to keep chapter_workbench.py within the GATE-11 size ratchet.

Fixes

  • AI Hub (and sibling AI dialogs) no longer crash if closed while a background probe is in flight (#1067). The AI Hub’s Ollama auto-probe, List-models, and Test-Connection actions run on a background thread and post their result back to the UI via wx.CallAfter. If you closed the dialog before the probe finished, the queued callback touched a StaticText/Button that wx had already torn down and raised RuntimeError: wrapped C/C++ object of type StaticText has been deleted. Those callbacks (and the same-shaped ones in AI Thesaurus lookup, AI Document Q&A ask, and the AI Model download) now check a shared dialog_alive guard and no-op when the dialog is gone. No behavior change when the dialog stays open; the result simply isn’t shown for a dialog you’ve already closed.

  • AI agent result and AI thesaurus dialogs no longer crash on open (#1091, #1094, #1097). Both AI > AI Thesaurus... and the agent/writing-assistant result dialog called QUILL’s modal-dialog gate without the label argument that names the modal region for screen readers – so either dialog crashed with TypeError: MainFrame._show_modal_dialog() missing 1 required positional argument: 'label' the moment it tried to appear, reported independently by three testers. Both now pass their dialog title as the label, matching the AI Hub and AI Model dialogs that already did. No behavior change beyond the crash going away; the dialogs were always meant to open.

0.9.0 Beta 3

The headline: One Editor, Every Format — the braille fix is on for everyone by default, and QUILL gains true rich text editing. Plus bug fixes driven by beta 2 community reports and eight small accessibility-first features. Rolls up on top of 0.9.0 Beta 2 (below).

One Editor, Every Format (the Beta 3 highlight)

  • The braille fix ships on by default — #616 and #813 are closed for every user. Beta 2 asked braille display owners to test an experimental combination; live JAWS + braille testing confirmed it works. QuillRichEdit — the same native Windows Rich Edit control QUILL always shipped, now with its Text Object Model wrapper — is THE editor surface, with the fix applied out of the box: text renders from braille cell 1 (no more “cell two” Word quirk) and selections show dots 7-8. Two plain checkboxes own the whole fix on Preferences > Braille: “Fix braille cell alignment and selection dots (recommended)” and “Hide editor border (required for braille cell alignment)” — live testing showed the visible border itself pushes braille out of cell 1, so the hidden border is part of the fix, not cosmetics. Both default on and are forced on for upgraders (old experimental overrides are dropped with a one-time notice); unchecking the border warns, specifically, that cell alignment will break. The whole Experimental-tab surface picker retired with this — the experiment is decided, and its engineering post-mortems are preserved in docs/engineering/editor-surface-history.md.
  • Rich mode: .rtf documents open as real rich text. An RTF now loads natively through the control’s Text Object Model — no conversion to Markdown on open, no fidelity loss on the round trip. Bold means bold: Ctrl+B applies genuine bold, Insert Heading 2 applies a real sized-and-bold heading, and Describe Formatting at Cursor answers from the live control (“Arial, 14 point, bold, centered”) instead of parsing markup. Search, spell, AI commands, read-aloud, bookmarks, notes, and braille all keep working — both modes expose the same plain text. Autosave snapshots rich documents as RTF bytes alongside the text snapshot, so crash recovery restores formatting, not just words. Where the native path is unavailable (macOS without the PyObjC extra, comtypes missing), .rtf opens converted exactly as before — never a blank editor, never a lost document.
  • Mode-polymorphic formatting: one command, one shortcut, the right effect for your format. In Markdown, Ctrl+B wraps **; in HTML, <strong>; in rich text, real bold via the native control. The Format menu’s font, size, color, highlight, and alignment commands route the same way. In plain text — which cannot carry bold, that’s the promise of .txt — the first formatting command asks once: treat as Markdown, convert to Rich Text, or stay plain (and then stays quiet).
  • The Document Format switcher. Format > Document Format… (also Ctrl+Shift+Grave, K; the command palette; and the new interactive Format status bar cell) moves the current document between Plain text, Markdown, HTML, Rich Text (RTF), and Word (.docx) mid-session, converting through the RichDocument bridge. Anything lossy warns first with the specific inventory of what won’t survive; a format switch retargets the file type on the next Save As — QUILL never silently writes a different format into the old extension. This closes #893 (making rich editing “discoverable”) a different way than originally proposed: rather than surfacing the old read-only Rich Text lens, that lens is retired outright and the switcher becomes the one, in-context way to move any document into real rich editing.
  • Editable rich Word documents (.docx) — with your original protected. A .docx can now open for real rich editing (headings, bold, fonts, colors, alignment), saved back as a genuine Word file. Because Word can carry things QUILL’s editor cannot (tables, images, comments, tracked changes, headers/footers), the open is honest: a clean file opens rich directly; a file with flagged features asks — open for reading and plain editing (the safe default), edit as Rich Text with the losses named specifically, or edit a copy. The first rich save over a flagged original writes a timestamped backup alongside it, and read-extract plus faithful export remain exactly as before when python-docx is unavailable.
  • macOS comes along — batteries included. The Mac editor is an NSTextView underneath, and a new PyObjC-based wrapper mirrors the Windows rich API over Cocoa’s text model — same commands, same heading sizes, same everything. The Mac app bundles the bridge: nothing for users to install (the py2app build now explicitly includes objc/AppKit/Foundation, which the import tracer used to miss because the imports are lazy — a fix that also makes the existing VoiceOver editor-role pin actually ship inside the .app instead of silently no-op’ing). Source installs get it via pip install "quill[mac]". If the bridge is ever absent, macOS degrades to converted rich: all shipped code, no new failure modes. VoiceOver hardware verification is the promotion gate — the release notes ask Mac users to report through Help > Report a Bug whether it works, good or bad.

Fixes

  • Browse Stations opens again. The Internet Radio station browser died before it could appear – a stray sizer alignment flag tripped a wx assertion in the dialog’s constructor – so the Browse Stations button and menu item both looked dead, in QUILL and in standalone Quill Radio alike. One flag removed; every Radio dialog path is now exercised headlessly in review.
  • The optional audio pack now downloads whole. “Audio: export, playback & chapters” in Download Optional Components used to fetch only the mpv player and MP3 chapter support, leaving FFmpeg to a second, surprise download the first time you exported compressed audio or recorded radio. Download now installs everything missing in one go – one progress bar, FFmpeg’s larger share weighted so the percentage moves honestly – and Remove cleans up both managed folders. Pieces already installed are skipped, and each piece still auto-fetches individually at first use if you never visit the dialog.
  • The Application Status page (Help > Status Page) is navigable again (#969). The page auto-refreshes every 2 seconds to keep task and download progress current, but the refresh rebuilt every list from scratch and forgot which row had keyboard focus — so pressing Down to move through the list got silently undone by the next tick, snapping focus back to the top before you could act on where you’d navigated. Each list now remembers and restores its focused row across a refresh, so the page is navigable the way any other accessible list is. Reported with exact repro steps that made the root cause obvious.
  • Narrator is now a first-class announcement target — no more double speech (#966). Two layers of fix. Detection: alongside the process check, QUILL now also reads Windows’ own API-level “Narrator is running” marker (the named NarratorRunning event, one cheap system call), so Narrator can never be missed. Speech: QUILL now talks to Narrator directly — announcements are raised as UI Automation notification events, the channel Narrator (and other UIA-aware readers) listens on, so you hear QUILL’s announcements in your own One Core voice. When that channel is unavailable, the message goes to the status bar; the SAPI self-voice never runs while any screen reader is live, “forced” announcements included — that bypass was the double-speech. JAWS and NVDA behavior is unchanged (their dedicated bridges still take priority). Reported by George Kerscher; the direct-Narrator channel needs on-device Narrator confirmation, which this beta asks for.
  • Install Starter Snippet Packs is now genuinely accessible (#959). The old checked-list dialog required pressing Space on an invisible checkbox with no feedback, and Enter on a merely-highlighted pack said “No packs selected.” It is now a plain multi-select list: arrow to a pack and press Enter to install it — one step — or hold Shift/Ctrl to select several; NVDA and JAWS announce selection state natively.
  • A macOS crash while creating a Notebook (#956). A window-activation event handler could crash the whole app when a wx string-array assertion fired during dialog teardown (“SystemError: ActivateEvent returned a result with an exception set”). Frame activation is a focus nicety, never worth the process: the handler is now fully contained, and the deferred focus-return tolerates a torn-down editor. Reported by Shannon Dyer.
  • Crash and feedback reports now say which beta you’re on (#967/#968 triage). Every 0.9.0 beta reported its version as just “0.9.0,” so a crash long since fixed (the Profiles and Features _LazyString crash, fixed in Beta 2 as #878/#879) looked like a live regression when reported from a Beta 1 install. Reports now carry the channel-annotated version (“0.9.0 Beta 2”) everywhere a version appears.
  • Portable updates left you with an unopened .zip and no in-app way to use it. Downloading an update while running portable always ended in the same “Open folder” / “Close” dialog an installer download gets — but a portable update is a ZIP, not something you run, so “Open folder” just handed you a raw archive to figure out yourself. Quill now offers Extract now for a downloaded portable update, which unzips it into a ready-to-run sibling folder and reveals that instead of the raw archive. (We also verified the underlying portable-vs-installed asset selection and version-check logic directly against a real portable build during this investigation — it correctly detects portable mode and downloads the portable ZIP, not the installer, matching what was reported. This fix addresses the actual friction: reaching for the ZIP still left you to extract it by hand.) Quill still doesn’t replace its own running files in place — you still copy your data folder over and swap folders yourself — but you no longer need to know how to unzip an archive first.
  • Pandoc EPUB (and other) imports could silently produce an empty document (#954). run_subprocess_safely decoded a subprocess’s output using the system’s default locale encoding rather than an explicit one — on a Windows machine whose locale defaults to a legacy code page like CP1252, Pandoc’s UTF-8 output could fail to decode, leaving stdout as None while still reporting success. The import then silently produced an empty document with no error at all. Output is now always decoded as UTF-8 (with errors="replace" so a genuinely non-UTF-8 byte never crashes or blanks the result, just shows a visible replacement character), and Pandoc conversion now explicitly raises if it ever gets no output instead of silently returning an empty result. Root-caused and reported with the exact fix by a community member — thank you.
  • Speech and Dictation crashed on open (#949, #950, #952, #953). Opening Tools > Speech > Speech and Dictation raised TypeError: SpeechSetupDialog.__init__() missing 2 required keyword-only arguments: 'kokoro_ok' and 'kokoro_can_install' every time, on both Offline and Online tabs — reported independently by four testers. open_speech_hub’s shared common_dict_kwargs was missing both keys; they’re now populated from is_kokoro_onnx_available()/kokoro_onnx_install_supported(), matching the existing vosk_ok/vosk_can_install pattern exactly.
  • A crash on macOS when a keystroke arrived before the first document existed (#951). _focus_is_in_document_surface() read self.editor/self.notebook directly instead of defensively, unlike the _documents_panel check right above it, so AttributeError: 'MainFrame' object has no attribute 'editor' could reach the global character hook. All three checks are now consistently getattr-guarded.
  • “Go to Entry in Notebook” and other shared tree-navigator dialogs no longer crash on open (#939). This was already fixed on main for #885 before Beta 2 shipped (verified: _show_tree_navigator no longer calls the invalid tree.Expand() on its hidden root) — documented here for anyone who filed or found the same report against a Beta 2 build.
  • The Kokoro-voices-unavailable error pointed at a menu item that doesn’t exist. Selecting a Kokoro voice without the kokoro-onnx package installed said “Tools > Speech > Install Kokoro ONNX will fetch it” — but that menu item was consolidated into Help > Download Optional Components during an earlier release and the error text was never updated to match. It now names the real location. Reported by a community member alongside the #949/#950/#952/#953 Speech and Dictation crash above (same offline-installer testing session).
  • On macOS, the G key stopped typing and opened Find instead (every keystroke, both cases). Find Next is bound to Cmd+G on macOS. Quill builds each menu item’s keyboard-shortcut hint by embedding it after a literal tab character, which wx parses as a real native accelerator, independent of Quill’s own dispatch. wx recognizes “Ctrl”, “Alt”, and “Shift” in that string, but not the literal word “Cmd” — it silently dropped the unrecognized modifier and kept the bare key, so “Find Next␉Cmd+G” quietly became a native accelerator for a bare, unmodified G, intercepting the letter everywhere in the app before it ever reached the document. This is the same bug class as #612 (the QUILL Key chord display), just triggered by a different unrecognized modifier word. Every macOS-only “Cmd+…” shortcut is affected, not just Find Next — the fix translates “Cmd” to “Ctrl” only in that one native-parsed slot (wx already renders “Ctrl” as the ⌘ symbol on macOS), so the visible shortcut still reads “Cmd+G” everywhere a person actually looks at it.
  • Portable updates that finish downloading but still show the old version after restart are not silently applying themselves — nothing does that today. A community member asked why QUILL still reported the old version after downloading and restarting. Portable updates have never auto-applied, on any QUILL release: the ZIP always lands in your updates folder and needs your own action to use it (previously a bare “Open folder” button; this release’s Extract Now, above, does the unzip step for you). If you’ve downloaded an update and restarted expecting it to just be there, that is the gap — copy your data folder into the newly extracted version and launch from there. We’ve reworded the in-app text and this release’s notes so “swap it into place” reads as the manual step it always was, not something QUILL does for you.
  • A per-machine install (Program Files, all users) no longer needs administrator elevation for SAPI, Narrator, or Rich Edit to work at full speed. comtypes writes generated COM wrappers to disk the first time it touches a given type library — SAPI’s voice, Narrator’s UIA notification bridge, and the Rich Edit rich-text object model each touch a different one. QUILL already redirected this cache to a writable per-user folder for SAPI, but Narrator’s and Rich Edit’s own comtypes calls only got that redirect if SAPI happened to run first in the same session — an accident of import order, not something QUILL actually guaranteed. Every comtypes call site now requests the redirect itself before touching comtypes, so none of the three ever depends on this happening to work first. Reported by Tyler Rodick on the BITS mailing list, who noticed startup was several seconds faster running as administrator under a Program Files install — the case this closes.
  • File > Notebook > Manage Versions no longer renders as a silent, unexplained blank list. A notebook with no saved Versions yet showed an empty list with no placeholder and no clue whether the feature was broken — a long-standing rough edge noted in planning (docs/planning/roadmap.md’s “Snapshots vs Versions”). The list now shows “(No versions saved yet)” and disables Rename/Delete until there’s something to act on, matching the “(No open documents in workspace)” pattern already used elsewhere in QUILL.
  • Insert > Date and Time’s submenu now actually opens (#974). Pressing Right Arrow on it jumped straight to the next top-level menu instead — because Quill builds its menu bar before Quillins (including the bundled insert-tools Quillin that supplies Insert Date / Insert Time / Insert Date and Time) finish loading, the submenu was silently empty every session, with nothing there for Right Arrow to open. Quillin loading now rebuilds the menu bar once it finishes, both at startup and after any enable/disable/install/remove/reload in the Quillins Manager, so contributed menu items — this submenu’s three entries, and any other Quillin’s Insert/Format/Search contributions — actually appear. Reported by Jayson Smith.
  • Quill no longer offers crash recovery for exits with nothing to diagnose (#940, #948). Two reports showed a log with only routine idle-sweep entries — no exception, no traceback — consistent with the process being killed externally (an OS shutdown, a forced close) rather than a QUILL bug. Quill now checks the log for real error evidence (ERROR, CRITICAL, or a traceback) before offering recovery at all; an inconclusive exit is treated the same as a clean one for the offer, and the “Quill detected an unclean exit” dialog no longer appears with nothing behind it. A genuine crash still logs an error and still offers recovery exactly as before — nothing about real crash detection changed. The autosave snapshot itself is never touched by this; only the prompt is suppressed.
  • Filed crash-recovery reports now include the evidence that justified them (#1013). A follow-up report’s filed body showed only routine log lines with no visible error, looking like a false-positive recovery offer. Root cause: the filed report bundles only the last ~6,000 characters of the log, while the offer-gating check above scans a much larger window (~256 KB) near the end of the file — so a genuine error early in that window could correctly justify the offer without ever appearing in what got filed. quill.core.recovery.find_error_evidence() now extracts the actual matching lines (with context) from that same window and includes them in the filed report, so a recovery report is self-explanatory instead of a mystery to whoever triages it.
  • macOS VoiceOver now announces what a control is for, not just its bare value (#1012). A community accessibility audit found two related gaps: many hand-built dialogs relied on a Windows-only convention where a neighboring StaticText label implicitly names a control — VoiceOver does not infer that association and announces nothing, or just the value; and every SpinCtrl/SpinCtrlDouble (used throughout Preferences and elsewhere) is a composite control on macOS whose inner text field needs its own name even when the composite itself was named, so a “named” spinner could still read as a bare number. A new shared helper (quill.ui.dialog_contract.set_accessible_name) names a control from its visible label (stripping the & mnemonic and trailing colon) and, for spin controls, names the inner field too. Swept across every affected dialog the audit identified — Preferences, Watch Folder profiles, Export/Import, GitHub dialogs, Quick Nav, Heading Organizer, List Manager, Regular Expression Helper, and dozens more.
  • A community bug report crashed QUILL on autosave with a UnicodeEncodeError. Autosave snapshots were written using the source document’s own encoding (e.g. “ascii” for a file read as Braille Ready Format) instead of UTF-8 — any character outside that narrower range (reproduced live with a U+2004 space introduced via abbreviation expansion) crashed the write outright. Recovery always reads a snapshot back as UTF-8 regardless of the source document’s encoding, so the writer now always writes UTF-8 too; a defensive try/except around the autosave call also keeps any future write failure from ever interrupting editing.
  • Radio and Podcasts could crash QUILL on every launch with AttributeError: 'MainFrame' object has no attribute 'frame'. The Radio and Podcast player controllers were being built before self.frame existed in MainFrame.__init__, so parenting them on the not-yet-created frame failed immediately. Both now initialize right after the frame is created.
  • Downloading the Braille Translation Pack didn’t make Translation appear without a restart. Same root cause as #974 (Quillin menu contributions): the download’s success handler refreshed menu-item state but never rebuilt the menu’s structure, so the Tools > Braille > Translation submenu — which only exists once the pack is installed — stayed absent until the next launch even though the pack was correctly installed on disk. The direct “Download Braille Translation Pack…” menu item also now routes through the Download Optional Components hub (matching the existing “Set Up Braille” flow) instead of dropping you back into the editor when the download finishes.
  • Back/forward-translating a large BRF file failed instantly, with no clear explanation. The whole document’s text was packed into a single command-line argument to the liblouis worker subprocess; Windows caps total command-line length at roughly 32,000 characters, so any sizeable BRF file (reproduced live on a ~450 KB hymnal) failed to even launch the worker. The request now travels over the worker’s stdin, which has no such limit, and a translation failure now also shows a visible dialog rather than only a spoken announcement that was easy to miss.
  • Fixed the AI Setup Wizard treating a working local Ollama as “not there,” and asking for a cloud API key instead. A never-configured install’s saved settings default to “Ollama,” which the wizard’s “remember what you already set up” logic mistook for proof you’d deliberately added Ollama — so on first run, the wizard silently marked Ollama as already added, dropped it from the Provider list, and left only key-requiring cloud providers to choose from. It now confirms a local Ollama server actually answers before treating it as configured, so Ollama shows up normally when it should. The AI Hub’s Provider tab had a related, simpler bug: the API key field stayed active for every provider regardless of whether one was needed, which is why Ollama’s key field looked required even though nothing ever enforced it; it now greys out correctly for Ollama and any other no-key provider.
  • Fixed the AI Setup Wizard’s Connect step landing you in the API key field instead of the Provider list, and its Remove button looking clickable before you’d selected anything. Reaching the “Connect your AI providers” step (or removing a provider from Added) could jump focus straight into an API key field when the next provider in line happened to need one — jarring, and easy to mistake for “Ollama needs a key too.” Focus now always starts on the Provider list itself. Remove selected also now stays disabled until you’ve actually selected an entry in the Added list, instead of looking usable the moment anything was added.
  • Ollama’s server address is now a real, editable field — for a self-hosted or LAN Ollama, not just localhost. The Setup Wizard always checked localhost and never let you point it anywhere else, even though the option to reach Ollama on another computer on your network was never actually wired up end to end. A new Ollama server address field on the Connect step (enabled only when Ollama is selected, defaulting to localhost) is used for the actual verify, model list, and Finish steps, so an Ollama running on another machine is a real, working choice.
  • Preferences now lands you on the right control when you switch tabs, not on a bare “Panel.” Using Ctrl+Tab or Ctrl+Shift+Tab to move between Preferences tabs (or clicking a different one) moved the visible page but left focus wherever it had been, so a screen reader sometimes announced just “Panel” instead of the new tab’s first field — for example, the “Enable experimental features” checkbox on the Experimental tab. Switching tabs now always moves focus to that tab’s first real control, the same routing every other tabbed dialog in QUILL already uses.
  • Audio Studio’s batch export finally has a working Cancel button. Generating audio for a folder of documents had no Cancel button at all, and pressing Escape just dinged — there was no way to stop a run short of killing QUILL outright. Cancel (and Escape) now work: whatever file is currently synthesizing finishes normally (so you never end up with a half-written audio file), and the run stops cleanly before starting the next one.
  • Audio Studio’s log file now shows the same progress you see on screen. The diagnostics log only ever recorded which document was up next, not the chunk-by-chunk progress within a document that the on-screen dialog already showed — so a log-only look at a run in progress (or a stalled one) told you far less than watching the screen did. The log now mirrors that same chunk-by-chunk detail.
  • Audio Studio: WAV exports get their own subfolder; MP3 still lands beside your document. Choosing WAV as your export format used to drop the (often large) file right next to your source document, the same place MP3 already lands. WAV output now goes into an Audio Output subfolder next to the document it came from — and when exporting recursively through a folder of subfolders, each subfolder gets its own Audio Output folder rather than everything funneling into one shared folder at the top.
  • QUILL now warns before Alt+F4 (or the window’s close button) silently abandons real work in progress. Closing QUILL while an Audio Studio export was running used to just exit immediately, mid-job, with no warning. QUILL now asks first — close and stop it now, or leave QUILL open so it can finish — and mentions File > Send to Tray as a way to keep it running quietly in the background instead. Routine background activity (search/replace, dictation, downloads) is unaffected; only real, hard-to-redo jobs like an Audio Studio export trigger the warning.
  • A background task’s very first status update could be silently dropped on a freshly-started QUILL. The coalescing logic that throttles rapid-fire progress updates (so a fast task doesn’t flood the UI with redraws) judged whether enough time had passed since the last update using a clock that starts counting from an arbitrary point rather than from zero — on a machine that had been running for under about 17 minutes, that arithmetic could come out negative, and the first update from a task never reached the screen. Now the first update from any task always gets through, regardless of how long the machine has been on.
  • Check for Updates could hand a Windows user the macOS download. When a tagged release publishes assets for only one platform — as happened when the Windows Beta 3 build failed in CI while the macOS build succeeded — the asset picker’s last-resort fallback returned any remaining asset rather than none, so a Windows client got pointed at the release’s lone .dmg. Asset selection now excludes every other platform’s installer extensions outright, and a release with nothing installable for the running platform is skipped entirely so Check for Updates falls back to the newest release that does have a matching build, instead of ever offering a foreign-platform link.

New

  • Internet Radio grows up: a Favorites Manager, a Recordings shelf, and recordings that survive a dropped connection. Three connected additions, identical in QUILL (Tools > Media) and standalone Quill Radio. Manage Favorites… organizes saved stations the way Podcasts organizes shows: folders of any depth (News/Morning style – old favorites files load unchanged), Move Up/Down inside a folder, Mark for Move plus Move Above/Move Below for long hops (the moved station joins the destination’s folder), a live search across names, countries, languages, tags, and folder names, folder rename (F2), and folder delete that walks its stations safely back to the top level – stations are never deleted with a folder. The Favorite Stations submenu mirrors the same nesting. Recordings… is the whole recording life cycle in one list – what is recording right now (size growing live), every finished file (newest first), and upcoming scheduled recordings with their times – with Play (through the shared player), Stop Recording, Open in Folder, and Remove. Recording now reconnects: when the internet hiccups mid-recording, ffmpeg first rides out short gaps itself, and if the connection truly dies the recorder waits and resumes into a numbered part file – attempts and spacing are yours to tune in Recording Settings, and every retry is announced. Starting any stream now also silences whatever else was playing (radio or podcast), so nothing ever double-plays. The standalone apps additionally gain QUILL’s own Command Palette (Ctrl+Shift+P), naturally scoped to each app’s commands.
  • Podcasts Phase 4: the podcast app grows up — pinned views, an Inbox, a Play Queue, transcripts, notes, local podcasts, and more. The Podcast Manager’s folder tree now leads with four pinned views: Favorites (mark any show from its context menu), New Episodes (every unplayed episode everywhere), Continue Listening (everything you’re partway through), and the Inbox — a personal triage layer that organizes episodes rather than shows: route a show to the Inbox and its new episodes appear there regardless of library folder; file episodes into your own nested Inbox folders, with the first manual filing per show remembered and auto-applied (and a Forget command to revert). A cross-show Play Queue (Play Next / Add to Queue on any episode) auto-advances when an episode ends and survives restarts, with fully keyboard-accessible reordering (Move Up/Down plus Mark-and-Move for long hops — the Interactive Rebase pattern). Search Everywhere searches shows, episodes, your notes, and already-fetched transcripts at once, jumping straight to the result; episode and show filters narrow the list live. Feed-provided transcripts (Podcasting 2.0; VTT/SRT/JSON) save to a file or open in the editor, cached for instant reopening and searching. Episode notes timestamp a moment in whatever’s playing; Enter on a note jumps playback there. Local podcasts turn your own audio files into shows (stored outside your synced data folder by construction), with optional watched folders that turn dropped files into new episodes. Subscribe to ACB Media Podcasts brings in ACB’s whole live directory in one idempotent, stream-only command. Always Sync backfills and downloads a show’s entire catalog (with an honest keep-all retention nudge); auto-trim silence and normalize loudness process finished downloads with the audiobook builder’s own ffmpeg passes; a volume boost control raises quiet audio live without disturbing the volume the Sleep Timer restores. Pause/stop/switching away now also saves your position (resume always knew how to read it; now it’s reliably written). Standalone QUILL Cast inherits every bit of this, plus its own menu items for local podcasts, watched folders, ACB Media, and episode notes.
  • Quill Radio and QUILL Cast: Radio and Podcasts as their own standalone apps. Sometimes you just want the radio on, or your podcast queue, without loading the whole editor. Quill Radio (run-quill-radio.bat, or python -m quill.apps.radio) and QUILL Cast (run-quill-cast.bat, or python -m quill.apps.podcasts) are small standalone apps with their own window, menu bar, and system tray icon. They are not forks: each one runs the exact same feature code QUILL itself uses — same station browser, same podcast manager, same download queue, same dialogs — through a thin shared shell (quill/ui/app_shell.py), and they read the same settings, favorites, and subscriptions from the same place, so what you subscribe to in QUILL Cast is subscribed in QUILL. Quill Radio’s menu bar covers Station (browse, add custom, link finder, favorites inline), Playback (with a live now-playing line), and Record (record now, schedule, settings); QUILL Cast covers Subscriptions (manager, add, OPML import/export, settings), Episode (play/pause, chapters), and Downloads (pause/resume all). Both apps open on a real, keyboard-first main panel — favorites (Radio) or subscribed shows (Cast) focused on launch, Enter to play or open the manager — with both tray icons mirroring the same radio/podcast sections QUILL’s own tray shows, plus Show and Exit; both carry Help > Open in Quill to launch the full editor when you need it. One standalone-only difference: QUILL Cast’s “Send Show Notes to Editor” copies the notes to the clipboard instead, since there is no editor buffer to send to. The installer now puts both apps in the Start Menu (with optional desktop icons) alongside QUILL itself.
  • Braille translation gets magical: auto-detected codes, one-command BRF conversion, and an engine that works everywhere. Three connected changes. You never pick a braille code again — Back-Translate to Text (Auto-Detect Code), now the first item on the Translation submenu, samples your document (or selection), tries UEB Grade 2, UEB Grade 1, legacy American (EBAE) Grade 1 and 2, and computer braille, scores how much each result reads like real English, and announces the winner (“Detected UEB Grade 2 (contracted)”) — you learn what your file is instead of being asked. When a file is uncontracted, QUILL says Grade 1 even though the contracted table would also have decoded it; the honest label wins ties. Convert BRF File to Document… goes from a .brf/.brl file on disk to a readable draft in one command — detect, back-translate, open — and because the draft is a normal document, Save As exports it to Markdown, HTML, Word, or plain text: BRF in, any format out. The engine now works from every kind of install — the translation worker previously required a separately installed Python liblouis binding that nothing actually installs, so running from source always failed with “liblouis is not installed” even with the pack present; the worker now falls back to the pack’s own bundled lou_translate engine (which has shipped with the pack all along), so installed, portable, and source-checkout builds all translate identically out of the box. Detection runs all candidate codes in a single worker launch, and every failure surfaces as a visible dialog, never just a missable spoken message. And File > Convert File… now accepts braille sources directly: .brf/.brl appear in its file picker with every other document type, dispatching to the auto-detecting braille path (Pandoc has no braille reader) — one converter dialog for everything, braille included, with the full output-format list (Word, HTML, EPUB, and the rest) available via a Markdown intermediate.
  • The Sticky Notes Browser: every note, one hotkey away — from anywhere in Windows. Tools > Sticky Notes Browser… (or a global hotkey you assign; see the next entry) opens a fast, keyboard-first finder: a search field filters your notes live as you type (titles and bodies, case-insensitive), Down-arrow drops into the results (newest first), Tab lands on a read-only multi-line preview of the selected note, and Edit (or Enter, or double-click) opens the full note editor. Invoked from another app via its global hotkey, QUILL’s window is restored first so the browser always actually appears.
  • Global Hotkeys: your keys, anywhere in Windows — bounded by a safety allowlist. Tools > Global Hotkeys… lets you bind system-wide key combinations (they must include Ctrl or Alt, so a bare letter can never be swallowed everywhere) to a curated list of commands that are genuinely safe to fire from outside QUILL: Radio and Podcasts transport (play/pause, stop, mute, volume), New Sticky Note, the Sticky Notes Browser, and Post to Mastodon (which only opens the compose dialog — nothing ever auto-sends). That list is the boundary by construction: nothing that edits a document or deletes anything can go global, whatever the settings file says, and a unit test guards the allowlist’s shape. Assignment captures the next combination you press; conflicts with another app’s hotkey are reported by name and simply stay inactive until you pick a different key; reassigning a key from one command to another steals it cleanly. Every global press speaks its outcome through the announcement engine, so triggering play/pause from your browser still tells you what happened. Dispatch runs through the same command gate as menus and the palette, so feature gating and the remote kill switch apply identically. Windows-only (the OS provides no macOS equivalent); your existing sticky-note hotkey keeps working untouched unless you override it here.
  • Help > Redeem Unlock Code… — signed, offline feature unlocking. Some capabilities ship locked until they’re ready for everyone and are enabled selectively for early testers and staged rollouts. An unlock code (starts with QUILL-) is verified by Ed25519 digital signature entirely on your machine — no network, nothing transmitted — and a valid code lifts its feature’s lock immediately, announced by name, persisting across restarts. Expired or tampered codes are rejected with a spoken reason. No code? Nothing changes: every standard feature works without one.
  • QUILL Sync: carry your settings and your projects between devices, without QUILL running a sync service. Two small, honest features rather than one large custom one. Sync via a folder — the existing “Where should QUILL store your data?” option (Help > Personalise QUILL, or the first-run wizard) is now documented as a sync strategy: point it at a folder OneDrive, Dropbox, Google Drive, or iCloud already keeps in sync, and your settings, snippets, dictionaries, and keymap travel with it — QUILL just writes to the folder, the sync client does the rest (don’t run QUILL from two devices against the same folder at the same time; there’s no conflict resolution for this path). Sync Folder with GitHub… (Tools menu) is new: point it at any folder — a writing project, a notes folder, anything — and QUILL commits, pulls, and pushes it over that folder’s own git remote, the same accessible commit/pull/push engine Vault’s “Sync Vault” already used, generalized to work on any folder. A folder that isn’t a git repository yet, or has no remote configured, is never silently set up — QUILL explains exactly what will run (git init, git remote add origin <url>) and asks first. Conflicts are listed by name, never auto-merged. Relies on your own git installation and its own credentials (SSH key or your system’s git credential manager), same as running git push from a terminal. We deliberately did not build a QUILL-branded sync engine with its own cloud accounts — the design considered and rejected is recorded in docs/engineering/sync-engine-history.md.
  • The Clipboard Collector is now system-wide (#964). Toggle it on and anything you copy — in your browser, your mail client, anywhere in Windows — appends to the open QUILL document, EdSharp-style, not just copies made inside QUILL. It watches the OS clipboard change counter (one cheap system call per tick, no clipboard access unless something actually changed), collects each distinct copy exactly once, and still autosaves after each collection as before. Also works on macOS via the pasteboard change count. Requested by Dean Martineau.
  • QUILL works as Thunderbird’s external editor (#965). Verified and documented: QUILL opens one process per file and exits when you close it — exactly the contract Thunderbird’s “External Editor Revived” add-on expects. The user guide’s new “Using QUILL as an external editor” section walks through the three-step setup. Requested by Martin Courcelles.
  • Quill can now add itself to PATH, so quill <file> works from any Command Prompt or PowerShell window (#941, opt-in). A tester wanted to open QUILL from a shortcut without typing the full install path, and separately noticed the user guide’s command-line section told everyone to run python -m quill — which only works from a source checkout, not an installed or portable copy. The Windows installer’s “Add Quill to PATH” task (unchecked by default, alongside the existing File associations tasks) adds the install directory to your per-user PATH — no elevation needed, and cleanly removed on uninstall — so quill resolves from a terminal or a shortcut’s Target field without the full install path. The user guide’s command-line examples now lead with quill.exe, the command every install and portable copy actually has.
  • Pull an Ollama model right from the AI Setup Wizard — no terminal required. Choosing your default local model used to tell you to open a terminal and run ollama pull <model> yourself. The Model step now shows which recommended models are already installed and lists the rest with a Pull model button that downloads it directly (with live progress), then selects it for you the moment it’s ready.
  • Temporary bookmark — a one-keystroke jump point with no dialog. Set with Ctrl+Shift+K, return with Alt+Shift+K. Unlike named bookmarks, nothing is ever asked and nothing is ever listed — it’s disposable scratch state for “come right back to exactly here,” and it does not persist between sessions by design.
  • Numbered quick bookmarks (0-9) — direct chords, no sub-mode. Set slot N with Alt+Shift+N; jump to it with Ctrl+Alt+Shift+N (N is any digit 0-9). One keystroke each way, no Quick Nav step in between. They persist the same way named bookmarks already do — no new storage, just a naming convention ("Quick 3", etc.) layered on the existing bookmark store, so they survive a restart.
  • Spell Check Word (Alt+F7) — an instant, single-word spelling check. Checks the word at the caret and offers the same suggestions/Add to Dictionary/Ignore choices as the right-click spelling menu, without opening the full-document Spell Check dialog. The keyboard equivalent of pressing F7 on a focused word in other word processors.
  • Accessible code folding. Fold a heading section or a fenced code block with Ctrl+Alt+Shift+F; jump between foldable regions with Alt+Shift+[ / Alt+Shift+]; see every fold at once with Ctrl+Alt+Shift+L (List Folds). Folding here is spoken state, never visual line-hiding — the document text is never touched, and ordinary arrow/word/line navigation is never intercepted, so nothing a screen reader user can reach is ever made silently unreachable.
  • Insert > Emoji… (Alt+Period) — browse or search all 3,781 standard emoji by name, keyword, a typed smiley like :) or <3, or the symbol itself, with a real spoken description of what each one looks like. No visual grid — that’s exactly the pattern that shuts screen reader users out of every other emoji picker — just a category list (Favorites and Recent first, then Unicode’s own nine groupings) and a named results list, same shape as QUILL’s other browse/search dialogs. Favorites and Recent (your last 30 inserted, automatically) sit at the top of the category list for one-step access to the emoji you actually use; an Add/Remove Favorites button acts on whatever’s selected, and un-favoriting or clearing Recent never removes the emoji from the picker itself — it’s still there under its usual category or search terms either way. Every description is original text QUILL generates itself from official Unicode data (not scraped from a picker site, for licensing reasons), so the catalog is fully self-hosted and works entirely offline once built.
  • Favorite folders — a short, curated list for instant access (Kurzweil-1000-style). Ctrl+Alt+Shift+A adds the current document’s folder to your favorites; Ctrl+Alt+Shift+R removes one; Ctrl+Alt+Shift+O opens the type-to-filter Quick Open dialog. Distinct from Windows’ recent-folders list, which tracks what you recently touched — a folder you use constantly but haven’t opened in months (the classic “boss might ask about this any minute” folder) stays in favorites regardless of recency.
  • Open From Favorite Folder — VSCode-style Quick Open, scoped to favorites. Type to filter by filename across every favorite folder at once, arrow to a match, Enter to open. Defaults to top-level files only so it stays instant even if a favorite hides a huge tree; tick the Include subfolders checkbox to search recursively when you need to. Quill has no single-project-root “workspace” the way VSCode does, so this is scoped to your curated favorites list rather than a whole tree.
  • Ranked spelling (Ctrl+Shift+L) — misspellings sorted by how often they recur. Kurzweil-1000-style: instead of reviewing errors in document order, the most-frequent misspelling comes first.
